Reading the Analects Through Self-as-an-End · Part III

Part III · In the Room
解论语 · 现场

Han Qin (秦汉) · Independent Researcher · 2026
DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.22656232 · Full PDF on Zenodo · CC BY 4.0

Cultivation as it actually ran

What this Part is doing

Volume Two built the instrument. Volume Three showed the man. This one shows the work being done.

Five books, and they contain the most concentrated teaching material in the Analects: the same question answered in opposite directions on the spot (11.22); three students asking what ren is and getting three different answers on one page (12.1–12.3); the hardest case in the book, argued twice (14.16–17, on Guan Zhong); and the sentence a stranger used to describe him, which this reading takes as the most accurate thing anyone said about him (14.38).

It also contains the fullest record of failure. Yan Hui dies here (11.8–11.11), and the teacher's grief is preserved without editing. Ran Qiu goes over to the Ji family and is disowned (11.17). Zilu is smiled at (11.26), and dies in the next generation.

Cultivation is not a method with guaranteed results. This Part is where that becomes plain.

1. The same question, opposite answers

The clearest single exchange in the book:

Zilu asked: "On hearing something, should one act on it at once?" The Master said: "Your father and elder brother are alive. How could you act on it at once?"

Ran You asked: "On hearing something, should one act on it at once?" The Master said: "Act on it at once."

Gongxi Hua said: "You asked whether one should act at once, and you said his father and elder brother were alive. Qiu asked the same, and you said to act at once. I am confused, and venture to ask." The Master said: "Qiu holds back, so I pushed him forward. You takes on two men's worth, so I pulled him back."

子路问：「闻斯行诸？」子曰：「有父兄在，如之何其闻斯行之？」冉有问：「闻斯行诸？」子曰：「闻斯行之。」公西华曰：「……赤也惑，敢问。」子曰：「求也退，故进之；由也兼人，故退之。」— 11.22

Three things are visible here that are visible nowhere else so plainly.

The answer tracks the asker, not the question. The words should one act at once are identical. What differs is who is standing there.

The same medicine is poison and cure. Ran You is cautious; he is pushed. Zilu overreaches; he is held. Neither answer is the true one, and both are correct.

And the principle is stated only because a third party was genuinely confused. Gongxi Hua is not a rhetorical device; he is a student who could not make sense of what he had just watched. Even the doctrine of fitting the teaching to the person is delivered fitted to the person.

Note what would have been lost had the compilers tidied this. Two contradictory answers to one question look like an error in a text. Keeping both, with the explanation, is what makes the method recoverable at all.

2. Three answers about ren, on one page

Books 12 opens with three students asking the same thing.

To Yan Hui — the one who was told he had held to ren for three months at a stretch:

"To discipline oneself and return to ritual is ren. … Do not look at what is contrary to ritual; do not listen to what is contrary to ritual; do not speak what is contrary to ritual; do not move against ritual." （非礼勿视，非礼勿听，非礼勿言，非礼勿动。）— 12.1

Four negations. A fence, and nothing inside it. What to look at, say and do in any actual situation is not specified — it is left to him.

To Zhonggong — the man of whom he said he could be given a seat facing south:

"Out of doors, behave as though you were receiving an important guest. Employ the people as though officiating at a great sacrifice. What you do not want done to you, do not do to others." — 12.2

Two working images and a rule. Concrete enough to be run by a man who will have to administer.

To Sima Niu — of whom the text says elsewhere that he talked too much and was anxious:

"The one with ren is hesitant in speech." "Hesitant in speech — is that all there is to ren?" "When doing it is hard, can one help being hesitant in speaking of it?" — 12.3

One clause, aimed at his particular fault.

Three answers, and none of them is the definition. Each is what this person needs to hear about ren now. And the compilers put all three on the same page — where they can be seen not to agree.

This is the strongest evidence in the book that there was no doctrine to transmit. A school with a definition of ren records the definition. This school recorded the prescriptions and left the definition out.

3. The hard case: Guan Zhong

The most demanding piece of reasoning in the Analects, and it runs across two chapters.

The facts. Guan Zhong served Prince Jiu. When Jiu was killed by his rival — who became Duke Huan of Qi — Guan Zhong did not die with his lord. He went over and served the man who had killed him, and made him the leading power of the age.

Zilu puts the objection: he did not die — was he not without ren? Zigong presses harder: he served the man who killed his master — surely that is not ren?

And the answer, twice, is the opposite of what a doctrine of loyalty requires:

"Duke Huan assembled the lords nine times without recourse to chariots and arms — that was Guan Zhong's doing. Such was his ren. Such was his ren." — 14.16

"Guan Zhong assisted Duke Huan to become leader of the lords and set the empire right, and the people to this day receive the benefit of it. But for Guan Zhong, we should be wearing our hair loose and folding our coats to the left. Was he to keep the fidelity of a common man or woman, hang himself in a ditch, and no one know of it?" — 14.17

Three things are being done here.

The criterion is stated, and it is not loyalty. What Guan Zhong is credited with is that a great many people were not destroyed. Assembled the lords without chariots and arms — the wars that did not happen.

Dying with one's lord is demoted, and named. The fidelity of a common man or woman — a small fidelity, kept at the cost of everything it could have protected. (And note where this leaves 1.7's deliver up one's person — a student had written into the definition of service the very thing his teacher here refuses.)

And Guan Zhong is not thereby exonerated. At 3.22 the same mouth says he was a small vessel, not frugal, and did not know ritual — screens at his gate, stands for inverted cups, the trappings of a ruler.

The two verdicts are not reconciled, and that is the method. Credited on ren, marked small in capacity, marked down on ritual — three axes, read separately, and no composite score issued. A man who saved a people can be a small man, and the text says both.

4. Knowing it cannot be done

A gatekeeper at the Stone Gate asks Zilu where he has come from, and on being told from Confucius, says:

"Is that the one who knows it cannot be done and does it anyway?" （是知其不可而为之者与？）— 14.38

A stranger, in one line, and this reading takes it as the most accurate description of him in the book.

Because it names the structure. Not: he believes it can be done. Not: he does it for the reward. He knows the odds and acts anyway — which means the acting is not conditional on the result.

And that is what makes the whole method possible. A teacher whose effort depends on outcomes will, sooner or later, produce the outcome by other means: press, condition, install. A teacher who has already given up on the guarantee can afford to leave the last step to the student.

The gibe is preserved as a gibe. Book 3 keeps a border official's tribute — Heaven will use your Master as a wooden bell (3.24). Book 14 keeps this. The school kept both, and the second is worth more.

5. The record of what did not work

Yan Hui dies in Book 11, and four chapters record what happened.

When Yan Yuan died, the Master said: "Alas! Heaven is destroying me! Heaven is destroying me!" — 11.9

When Yan Yuan died, the Master wailed for him without restraint. His followers said: "Master, you are giving way." He said: "Am I giving way? If not for this man, for whom?" — 11.10

No composure. No teaching drawn from it. And the students telling him he had lost his self-possession is left in.

And then the harder chapter. Yan Hui's father asks for the Master's carriage to be sold to pay for an outer coffin. He refuses — his own son had none either, and it is not fitting for a man who has held office to go on foot. The disciples give Yan Hui a grand funeral anyway. The Master says: "Hui treated me as a father, and I have not been able to treat him as a son. It was not I — it was you, my young men." (11.11)

They disobeyed him at the funeral of the student he loved most, and the record keeps his objection and their disobedience.

Ran Qiu goes further. As steward to the Ji family he increases their wealth by taxation, and:

"He is no follower of mine. My young men, beat the drum and attack him — that would be permitted." — 11.17

A student is disowned in the text.

And Zilu is smiled at (11.26), and dies in Wei in the next generation, stopping to retie his cap.

None of this is edited out. A school assembling a founder's authority does not preserve the funeral it lost control of, the student who went over to the enemy, or the teacher weeping past what his own students thought decent.

6. What this Part establishes

One: there was no doctrine. Three answers about ren on one page, two opposite answers to one question, and an explicit statement that the difference is the point. What was transmitted was a way of reading people, not a set of propositions.

Two: the criterion is whether people were preserved. The Guan Zhong case settles it. Asked to choose between a rule of loyalty and the survival of a population, he chose without hesitation — and did not pretend the man was thereby made admirable.

Three: the method does not come with a guarantee. This is the Part where it fails — where the best student dies, the funeral goes wrong, a student defects, and the teacher's grief is recorded without a lesson attached.

And the gatekeeper's line explains why the failure does not undo it. The one who knows it cannot be done and does it anyway is describing a man whose practice never depended on it working.

Appendix · The chapters of Books 11–15

Every chapter, with its position and the reason for it.

Reading the entries. Anchor = this reading builds on it; (core) = load-bearing; (probable) / (weak) = admitted with lower confidence. Non-anchor is not a claim that a passage is wrong. Split judgment = units of one chapter judged separately.

Where a reading is opposed, it is stated in the entry.

Book 11 · 先进 Xian Jin

The book of the students — and of what happened to them. Yan Hui dies here, in four consecutive chapters that record grief without a lesson attached. Ran Qiu is disowned. Zilu is smiled at, and told he will not die well. And the most important pedagogical exchange in the Analects (11.22) sits in the middle of it.

It is also the book where the students overrule their teacher (11.11) — the prototype of everything this reading calls the downward step, caught in the act at a funeral.

11.1

"Those who came to ritual and music first were rustics; those who came to them afterwards were noble men. If I had to use them, I should follow those who came first."

子曰：「先进于礼乐，野人也；后进于礼乐，君子也。如用之，则吾从先进。」

Anchor.

Two orders of arrival. Learn ritual and music, then take office — the man had the substance before he had the position. Take office, then learn ritual and music — the forms came as the furnishing of a station already held.

He takes the first. Which is 3.8 applied to careers: the plain ground comes before the painting.

11.2

"Of those who followed me in Chen and Cai, none now comes to my door." (Followed by:) Virtuous conduct: Yan Yuan, Min Ziqian, Ran Boniu, Zhonggong. Speech: Zai Wo, Zigong. Government: Ran You, Jilu. Culture and learning: Ziyou, Zixia.

子曰：「从我于陈、蔡者，皆不及门也。」德行：颜渊、闵子骞、冉伯牛、仲弓。言语：宰我、子贡。政事：冉有、季路。文学：子游、子夏。

Split: the saying is an anchor (probable); the list is editorial.

The saying is an old man's remark about scattering — those who were with him in the worst of it are no longer around (with let us go home, 5.22; I have declined, 7.5).

The list of four faculties and ten disciples is a later annotation. It sorts the students into departments — and the sorting is exactly what the teaching does not do. He assessed people one at a time, on several axes, and refused composite grades (5.7, 5.19). A faculty list is a composite grade.

(Not opposed. It is a school organizing its own memory, and it is useful information. It is simply not his.)

11.3 — (See 11.2. Some editions number the list separately; this reading treats it as one chapter.)

11.4

"Hui is no help to me. There is nothing I say that he does not delight in."

子曰：「回也非助我者也，于吾言无所不说。」

Anchor (core). The clearest statement that cultivation requires resistance.

His best student accepts everything, and this is a complaint.

Because a student who never pushes back cannot lift the teacher — cannot do what Zixia did at 3.8 (it is Shang who draws me out), or Zhonggong at 6.2 (Yong's words are right), or Zigong at 5.9.

Which tells us what he thought the relation was. Not transmission into a receptacle. Something that required the other party to be a source too — and total agreement, however sincere, leaves him with nothing coming back.

11.5

"What a filial son Min Ziqian is! No one takes exception to what his parents and brothers say of him."

子曰：「孝哉闵子骞！人不间于其父母昆弟之言。」

Anchor (probable).

The test of filial conduct is placed outside the man. Not what he says of himself, not the observances he performs — that when his own family praise him, nobody who knows the household disputes it.

The people best placed to contradict him do not (with examine where he rests, 2.10; long acquainted, he still showed them respect, 5.17).

11.6

Nan Rong repeated the lines about the white sceptre three times a day, and Confucius gave him his elder brother's daughter in marriage.

南容三复白圭，孔子以其兄之子妻之。

Anchor (probable).

The lines are from the Odes: a flaw in a white sceptre may be ground away; a flaw in one's speech can be done nothing about.

He repeated them three times a day — that is, he was working on his own mouth, daily and without being watched.

And this is the file behind the prognosis at 5.1 (when the state has the way he will not be discarded; when it has not, he will escape punishment). A man that careful about what he says survives both kinds of government. The assessment is again underwritten with a marriage.

11.7

Ji Kangzi asked which of his disciples loved learning. Confucius replied: "There was Yan Hui, who loved learning. Unfortunately his life was short and he died. Now there is no one."

季康子问：「弟子孰为好学？」孔子对曰：「有颜回者好学，不幸短命死矣，今也则亡。」

Anchor (core). The same answer as 6.3, given to a different person on a different occasion.

Two independent records of one answer. 6.3 (to Duke Ai) includes the definition — he did not transfer anger; he did not repeat an error. This one, to the head of the Ji family, is bare.

Which is itself worth noting: to the duke he explains what he means by learning; to Ji Kangzi he does not.

Yan Hui's death (11.8–11.11)

Four consecutive chapters. Read them in order.

11.8

When Yan Yuan died, Yan Lu asked for the Master's carriage, to sell it for an outer coffin. The Master said: "Talented or not, each of us speaks of his own son. When Li died, he had an inner coffin and no outer one. I did not go on foot in order to give him one — because, having followed in the train of the great officers, I may not go on foot."

颜渊死，颜路请子之车以为之椁。子曰：「才不才，亦各言其子也。鲤也死，有棺而无椁。吾不徒行以为之椁。以吾从大夫之后，不可徒行也。」

Anchor (core). The refusal begins here.

The father of the dead student asks him to sell his carriage. And he refuses, giving two reasons — and both of them decline to make Yan Hui exceptional.

My own son had no outer coffin either. Talented or not, each of us speaks of his own son — he will not treat the loss of a brilliant student as requiring more than the loss of an ordinary one.

And I may not go on foot — a plain statement about what his standing requires.

Neither reason is grand. That is the point: the man he called irreplaceable will not get an irregular funeral because he was irreplaceable.

11.9

When Yan Yuan died, the Master said: "Alas! Heaven is destroying me! Heaven is destroying me!"

颜渊死。子曰：「噫！天丧予！天丧予！」

Anchor (core).

Nothing but the cry, twice.

No consolation, no resignation to fate, no lesson. Compare 6.10, at Bo Niu's sickbed — it is fate — where the same wall is reached and the same nothing is said past it.

11.10

When Yan Yuan died, the Master wailed for him without restraint. His followers said: "Master, you are giving way." He said: "Am I giving way? If not for this man, for whom?"

颜渊死，子哭之恸。从者曰：「子恸矣！」曰：「有恸乎？非夫人之为恸而谁为？」

Anchor (core).

恸 means grief past what the form allows — and the followers say so, quoting his own teaching back at him (grief without injury, 3.20).

And he does not accept the correction. If not for this man, for whom?

Two things are preserved here that a tidying hand would have removed: the students telling their teacher he has lost his composure, and the teacher declining to regain it.

11.11

When Yan Yuan died, the disciples wished to bury him grandly. The Master said: "You may not." They buried him grandly. The Master said: "Hui treated me as a father, and I have not been able to treat him as a son. It was not I — it was you, my young men."

颜渊死，门人欲厚葬之，子曰：「不可。」门人厚葬之。子曰：「回也视予犹父也，予不得视犹子也。非我也，夫二三子也。」

Anchor (core). The prototype event of this whole reading.

He said no. They did it anyway.

Out of love, out of respect, out of the conviction that the man deserved better — and against an explicit instruction from the person whose judgment they were honouring.

And his response is not anger. It is a statement of what was lost: Hui treated me as a father, and I have not been able to treat him as a son. The relation between them had a shape, and the grand funeral broke it.

Then: it was not I — it was you. He declines responsibility for the act done in his name.

Everything this book describes as the downward step is here in miniature: students who mean well, who have understood the value of the thing, and who complete on their teacher's behalf a judgment he had explicitly made the other way. The remainder was not left; it was filled in, at a funeral, by people who loved him.

11.12

Jilu asked about serving the spirits. The Master said: "You are not yet able to serve people — how could you serve the spirits?" "May I ask about death?" "You do not yet know life — how could you know death?"

季路问事鬼神。子曰：「未能事人，焉能事鬼？」曰：「敢问死。」曰：「未知生，焉知死？」

Anchor (core). The boundary between the knowable and the not.

Two questions, and both are folded back onto what is at hand. Spirits → people. Death → life.

This is not a denial that there are spirits, and not a refusal to think about death. It is a redirection: the question you are asking runs past a question you have not answered.

And it is the same discipline as respect the spirits and keep them at a distance (6.22) and he did not speak of prodigies or spirits (7.20) — knowing is keeping the boundary, not crossing it.

Note also who is asking. Zilu, who acts before he judges, is being told: the ground you are standing on is not finished yet.

11.13

Min Ziqian, standing by, was upright; Zilu, unbending; Ran You and Zigong, affable. The Master was pleased. "A man like You will not die a natural death."

闵子侍侧，訚訚如也；子路，行行如也；冉有、子贡，侃侃如也。子乐。「若由也，不得其死然。」

Anchor (core).

He is pleased at four students being four different things — upright, unbending, affable — each showing his own quality. (With the three aspirations of 5.26, where the grammar of each answer placed the man.)

And then, in the same breath, the worry: unbending has a cost. A man like You will not die a natural death.

Which is the whole of his method in two sentences: pleased that each is himself, and clear-eyed about where each man's own quality will take him. Zilu died in a coup in Wei.

11.14

The men of Lu were rebuilding the treasury. Min Ziqian said: "Why not leave it as it was? What need to rebuild?" The Master said: "That man does not speak often; when he speaks, he hits the mark."

鲁人为长府。闵子骞曰：「仍旧贯，如之何？何必改作？」子曰：「夫人不言，言必有中。」

Anchor.

The praise is for economy, not for silence. He does not speak often; when he speaks, he hits the mark — the value is in hitting, and speaking little is what makes it visible (with halting in speech 4.24, slow of speech, near to ren 13.27).

11.15

The Master said: "What is You's zither doing at my door?" The disciples then treated Zilu without respect. The Master said: "You has ascended to the hall. He has simply not entered the inner room."

子曰：「由之瑟，奚为于丘之门？」门人不敬子路。子曰：「由也升堂矣，未入于室也。」

Anchor (core). Not a joke — and both halves are teaching.

The first remark is a real criticism, delivered through the zither: something in how Zilu played carried the same hardness that is his fault elsewhere.

And then the students take it as licence to look down on him — and are corrected immediately. He has ascended to the hall; he has simply not entered the inner room.

Two things are done in four sentences. The fault is named to the man. And the standing of the man is defended against the people who overheard it. Ascended to the hall is high praise; the criticism was a location, not a verdict.

A teacher who corrects in public has to do this second thing, or the correction becomes something else.

11.16

Zigong asked: "Which is the better, Shi or Shang?" The Master said: "Shi goes too far; Shang does not go far enough." "Then Shi is the better?" "To go too far is the same as not going far enough."

子贡问：「师与商也孰贤？」子曰：「师也过，商也不及。」曰：「然则师愈与？」子曰：「过犹不及。」

Anchor (core). The clearest statement of two-sided calibration.

Zigong assumes a scale with more and less on it, and that more is better. The answer refuses the scale. Both are deviations, in opposite directions, and neither is nearer.

Which is what "the mean" means in this reading — not the arithmetic middle, but not being captured by either end (with 4.10, 6.18, 6.29).

11.17

The Ji family were richer than the Duke of Zhou, and Qiu gathered taxes for them and added further to their wealth. The Master said: "He is no follower of mine. My young men, beat the drum and attack him — that would be permitted."

季氏富于周公，而求也为之聚敛而附益之。子曰：「非吾徒也，小子鸣鼓而攻之，可也！」

Anchor (core). A student disowned, in the text.

Ran Qiu — the cautious one, the capable administrator, one of the ten named in 11.2 — is squeezing the people to make an already immensely rich usurping family richer.

And the disowning is public and unqualified. No follower of mine. Beat the drum and attack him.

Two things this establishes.

The criterion is not membership. Being of the school does not protect a man who is doing this. And the anger is proportionate to the offence, not to the relationship.

And it locates precisely what he objected to. Not that Ran Qiu served the Ji family — he had served them for years with his teacher's knowledge. That he used the position to take from the people in order to add to the wealth of the rich — which is 6.4 exactly, inverted: help the urgent; do not add to the wealth of the rich.

11.18

Chai is simple; Shen is slow; Shi is extreme; You is coarse.

柴也愚，参也鲁，师也辟，由也喭。

Anchor (probable).

Four students, four faults, named flatly — including Zengzi (slow), whose material fills much of this book.

Assessment as a working practice: knowing a student is knowing where he is off. (With 4.7, observe the faults and you know the ren.)

11.19

"Hui is close to it, and often in want. Ci does not accept his lot and goes into trade; his guesses often hit."

子曰：「回也其庶乎，屡空。赐不受命，而货殖焉，亿则屡中。」

Anchor (probable).

Two students set side by side, and neither is scored. Hui is near the way — and poor. Ci is not resigned to poverty, trades, and is very good at it.

No verdict is issued on either. The observation is exact and the ranking is withheld — which is what multi-axis assessment looks like when the axes point in different directions.

(This chapter is also the file behind 1.15: the man asking there about being poor without fawning and rich without arrogance is the man described here.)

11.20

Zizhang asked about the way of the good man. The Master said: "He does not tread in the tracks, and he does not enter the inner room either."

子张问善人之道。子曰：「不践迹，亦不入于室。」

Anchor.

Read here as being about the cultivator — the one who brings others on — the answer gives two constraints at once.

Do not tread in the tracks: do not copy a predecessor's method; it must be fitted to the person in front of you (7.8, 11.22).

And that alone does not get you to the inner room either: refusing the tracks is not sufficient. Not following a formula is where the work starts, not where it ends.

11.21

"Someone whose discourse is solid — is he a noble person, or only grave in appearance?"

子曰：「论笃是与，君子者乎？色庄者乎？」

Anchor (probable).

Solid speech does not settle it, because gravity of manner can produce solid speech too.

Which is the standing warning of this reading: manner is a signal and never a verdict (with 1.3, 5.25, and the xiangyuan of 17.13). The question is left open — he does not say which this one is.

11.22

Zilu asked: "On hearing something, should one act on it at once?" The Master said: "Your father and elder brother are alive. How could you act on it at once?" Ran You asked: "On hearing something, should one act on it at once?" The Master said: "Act on it at once." Gongxi Hua said: "You asked whether one should act at once, and you said his father and elder brother were alive. Qiu asked the same, and you said to act at once. I am confused, and venture to ask." The Master said: "Qiu holds back, so I pushed him forward. You takes on two men's worth, so I pulled him back."

子路问：「闻斯行诸？」子曰：「有父兄在，如之何其闻斯行之？」冉有问：「闻斯行诸？」子曰：「闻斯行之。」公西华曰：「……赤也惑，敢问。」子曰：「求也退，故进之；由也兼人，故退之。」

Anchor (core). The clearest demonstration of the method in the Analects.

Discussed in full in the essay above. Three things in one exchange: the answer tracks the asker; the same medicine is poison and cure; and the principle is stated only because a bystander was genuinely confused.

And the chapter's survival is itself evidence. Two contradictory answers to one question look like a defect in a text. Keeping both, with the explanation attached, is what makes the method recoverable — and a compiler smoothing for consistency would have dropped one.

11.23

The Master was in danger at Kuang, and Yan Yuan fell behind. The Master said: "I thought you were dead." He said: "While you are alive, how should I dare to die?"

子畏于匡，颜渊后。子曰：「吾以女为死矣！」曰：「子在，回何敢死？」

Anchor (core).

Not an ornament of devotion. While you are alive, how should I dare to die is a statement about what he is for: there is something not yet finished between them, and he will not spend his life before it is.

And the teacher's line is the plainer of the two. I thought you were dead — said to a student he later called irreplaceable, and recorded without adornment.

11.24

Ji Ziran asked: "May Zhongyou and Ran Qiu be called great ministers?" The Master said: "I thought you would ask about something else, and you ask about You and Qiu. What is called a great minister serves his ruler by the way, and stops when he cannot. As things are, You and Qiu may be called stopgap ministers." "Then will they simply follow their master?" "In the killing of a father or a ruler, they would not follow."

季子然问：「仲由、冉求可谓大臣与？」子曰：「……所谓大臣者，以道事君，不可则止。今由与求也，可谓具臣矣。」曰：「然则从之者与？」曰：「弑父与君，亦不从也。」

Anchor (core). The definition of a minister, and a floor beneath it.

Serve by the way, and stop where you cannot. 不可则止 — the same clause Ziyou compressed at 4.26, and the same instruction given about friends at 12.23. Service is conditional, and the condition is the way.

And his own students do not meet the standard — stopgap ministers, said to a member of the family employing them.

Then the floor. Asked whether they would simply comply, he does not defend their independence in general. He names the one thing they would not do: take part in killing a father or a ruler.

Which is a remarkably low bar, stated as a bar — and it is his honest estimate of two men he taught for decades.

11.25

Zilu sent Zigao to be steward of Bi. The Master said: "You are ruining another man's son." Zilu said: "There are people there, and altars of state — why must one read books before it counts as learning?" The Master said: "This is why I detest the glib."

子路使子羔为费宰。子曰：「贼夫人之子。」子路曰：「有民人焉，有社稷焉，何必读书，然后为学？」子曰：「是故恶夫佞者。」

Anchor (core).

You are ruining another man's son. The objection is that Zigao is not ready — and the phrasing puts the boy's father in the room.

And Zilu's reply is not stupid. It is, in fact, a good argument — and one his teacher makes himself elsewhere (learning is not books: 1.6, 1.14, 6.3).

Which is why the answer is what it is. This is why I detest the glib: a sound principle has been produced to cover a decision that was made on other grounds. The argument is true and it is not the reason.

And what is being protected is the person, not the school's placement. A steward's post at Bi — the Ji family's own town — is worth having. He would rather lose it than send someone unripe.

11.26

Zilu, Zeng Xi, Ran You and Gongxi Hua were sitting with him. The Master said: "I am a day or so older than you, but do not let that stop you. You are always saying, 'We are not known.' If someone did know you, what would you do?"

Zilu answered at once… "A state of a thousand chariots… in three years I could give the people courage and a sense of direction." The Master smiled at him.

[Ran You: a small territory, three years, sufficiency for the people — ritual and music must wait for a better man. Gongxi Hua: not that he can do it, but he would like to learn — a minor assistant at the ancestral rites.]

Zeng Xi was playing the zither. He set it down, rose, and said: "I differ from these three." "What harm? Each is telling what he is set on." "In late spring, the spring clothes being ready, with five or six capped young men and six or seven boys, to bathe in the Yi, feel the breeze on the rain-altar terrace, and come home singing." The Master sighed and said: "I am with Dian."

子路、曾皙、冉有、公西华侍坐……子路率尔而对曰：「……可使有勇，且知方也。」夫子哂之。……曰：「莫春者，春服既成，冠者五六人，童子六七人，浴乎沂，风乎舞雩，咏而归。」夫子喟然叹曰：「吾与点也！」

Anchor (core), with a layer to strip.

The setting is the method. I am a day or so older than you, but do not let that stop you — the seniority is put aside before the question is asked. And the question is aimed at what they actually complain about: you are always saying you are not known.

Four answers, and the grammar of each places the man (as at 5.26). Zilu answers first and fast — and is smiled at, not corrected. Ran You reduces the scale and defers ritual and music to a better man. Gongxi Hua reduces it further and asks only to learn.

Zeng Xi is doing something else entirely: he is playing music, and he sets it down before answering. And his answer has no office in it, no state, no three-year plan — a spring afternoon, a river, a walk home singing.

And I am with Dian.

What the assent is to, on this reading: the other three described positions they would hold. Zeng Xi described a condition people are in — several men, some boys, a river, and nobody being administered. It is the same shape as the old at ease, friends trusting, the young cherished (5.26): not what I would do, but what it would be like where the thing had worked.

The layer to strip: the length and literary finish of Zeng Xi's passage — the zither set down, the spring clothes ready — are unlike anything else in the text, and the Zeng school's hand is a reasonable suspicion. The exchange is not doubted. The polish is noted.

Book 11 in sum. Fourteen core anchors.

And it is the book that shows the method and its limits in the same pages. 11.22 is the method working — the same question, two answers, and the reason given. 11.11 is the method being overridden — by his own students, out of love, at a funeral.

Between them stands 11.4, which explains why the second could happen at all: Hui is no help to me; there is nothing I say that he does not delight in. A teacher who needs to be pushed back against had, in this room, students who mostly did not.

Book 12 · 颜渊 Yan Yuan

The book opens with three students asking what ren is, and getting three different answers on one page. That arrangement is the strongest evidence in the Analects that there was no doctrine to transmit — a school with a definition records the definition; this one recorded the prescriptions.

The rest of the book is government, and it runs on one move made repeatedly: a ruler asks how to manage the people, and the answer turns back onto the ruler. Ji Kangzi asks three times (12.17–12.19) and is redirected three times.

The three answers about ren (12.1–12.3)

12.1

Yan Yuan asked about ren. The Master said: "To discipline oneself and return to ritual is ren. If for a single day a man could do this, the whole world would turn to ren. Becoming ren proceeds from oneself — how could it proceed from others?" Yan Yuan said: "May I ask the details?" The Master said: "Do not look at what is contrary to ritual; do not listen to what is contrary to ritual; do not speak what is contrary to ritual; do not move against ritual." Yan Yuan said: "Slow though I am, I will put these words into practice."

颜渊问仁。子曰：「克己复礼为仁。一日克己复礼，天下归仁焉。为仁由己，而由人乎哉？」颜渊曰：「请问其目。」子曰：「非礼勿视，非礼勿听，非礼勿言，非礼勿动。」颜渊曰：「回虽不敏，请事斯语矣。」

Anchor (core).

Two things are given, and the second is the famous one.

Becoming ren proceeds from oneself. No one else is required, and no one else can do it. (With I want ren, and ren is here, 7.29; you have drawn a line, 6.12.)

And then the four negations. Do not look, listen, speak, move against ritual.

Four "do nots" that mark a boundary and specify nothing inside it. What to look at, what to say, what to do in any actual situation is not supplied — that is left to the man in front of him. A fence, and open ground within it.

The misreading we oppose: the later practice of filling the fence in item by item, until conduct became a checklist to be matched against. What was a boundary with judgment inside it became a list with compliance inside it.

Note the limit of this objection. The fault is not that anyone made a list. A list can remind, can scaffold, can be argued with. The fault is in taking it as exhaustive, unrevisable and not to be questioned — because at that point the thing this chapter left to Yan Hui is gone.

12.2

Zhonggong asked about ren. The Master said: "Out of doors, behave as though you were receiving an important guest. Employ the people as though officiating at a great sacrifice. What you do not want done to you, do not do to others. Then there will be no resentment against you in the state, and none in the family." Zhonggong said: "Slow though I am, I will put these words into practice."

仲弓问仁。子曰：「出门如见大宾，使民如承大祭。己所不欲，勿施于人。在邦无怨，在家无怨。」仲弓曰：「雍虽不敏，请事斯语矣。」

Anchor (core).

Two working images and a rule. As though receiving an important guest — every person met is treated as one would treat the one guest who matters. As though officiating at a great sacrifice — using people is done with the gravity one brings to the largest rite.

And then reciprocity, in its negative form: what you do not want, do not do.

Note who is being answered. This is the man of whom he said he could be given a seat facing south (6.1) — a man who will have to administer. What he receives is operable: two images he can carry into a room and a rule he can apply while doing it.

Compare Yan Hui's answer. Yan Hui got a fence and nothing inside it. Zhonggong gets something he can run. Same question, two men, two prescriptions.

12.3

Sima Niu asked about ren. The Master said: "The one with ren is halting in his speech." "Halting in speech — is that what is called ren?" The Master said: "When doing a thing is hard, can one help being halting in speaking of it?"

司马牛问仁。子曰：「仁者，其言也讱。」曰：「其言也讱，斯谓之仁已乎？」子曰：「为之难，言之得无讱乎？」

Anchor (core).

One clause — and the student objects that it is too little.

And the reply does not enlarge the definition. It explains the connection: when doing a thing is hard, can one help being halting in speaking of it? Speech runs ahead of action when the action has not been felt as difficult.

Sima Niu is described in the histories as talkative and agitated. The answer is aimed at him.

The three together

One question, three answers, and none of them is the definition.

And the compilers put all three on the same page, where they cannot be missed. A school with a doctrine of ren records the doctrine. This one recorded the prescriptions, side by side, unreconciled.

12.4

Sima Niu asked about the noble person. The Master said: "The noble person does not grieve and does not fear." "Not grieving and not fearing — is that what is called a noble person?" The Master said: "Looking within and finding nothing amiss — what is there to grieve about, what to fear?"

司马牛问君子。子曰：「君子不忧不惧。」曰：「不忧不惧，斯谓之君子已乎？」子曰：「内省不疚，夫何忧何惧？」

Anchor (core).

Again a short answer, again the same student objects, and again the second answer supplies the ground rather than more content.

Looking within and finding nothing amiss. The absence of grief and fear is not a temperament; it is what follows when there is nothing being covered.

And 12.5 explains why this was said to this man.

12.5

Sima Niu said in distress: "Everyone has brothers; I alone have none." Zixia said: "I have heard it said: death and life are allotted; wealth and honour rest with Heaven. If the noble person is reverent and without lapse, respectful to others and observant of ritual, then all within the four seas are his brothers. Why should a noble person be distressed at having no brothers?"

司马牛忧曰：「人皆有兄弟，我独亡。」子夏曰：「商闻之矣：死生有命，富贵在天。君子敬而无失，与人恭而有礼，四海之内皆兄弟也。君子何患乎无兄弟也？」

Anchor.

And this chapter is why 12.4 says what it says. Sima Niu is grieving — his brother Huan Tui was in revolt and heading for ruin. The teacher's the noble person does not grieve was said to a man in the middle of grief.

Which changes how that answer reads. Not a definition offered in the abstract, but something said to someone who needed a place to stand.

The consoling here is Zixia's, and it is the school at its best — all within the four seas are brothers is a student's line, and a good one. The chapter does not anchor on Zixia's formula; it anchors on the situation it discloses.

12.6

Zizhang asked about clear-sightedness. The Master said: "When slander that soaks in like water and accusations that cut like flesh-wounds get nowhere with you — that may be called clear-sighted. When slander that soaks in and accusations that cut get nowhere with you, that may be called far-sighted indeed."

子张问明。子曰：「浸润之谮，肤受之愬，不行焉，可谓明也已矣。浸润之谮，肤受之愬，不行焉，可谓远也已矣。」

Anchor (core).

The two kinds named are exactly the two that work. Slander that soaks in — not one accusation but a slow saturation, no single instance of which is refutable. And accusation that cuts to the flesh — the urgent, personal charge that demands an immediate response.

A single defamation is easy to see. These two are the forms that get past.

And clear-sightedness is defined by what fails to work on you — negative, as usual, and stated as a capacity rather than a virtue.

12.7

Zigong asked about government. The Master said: "Enough food, enough weapons, and the people's trust." Zigong said: "If one had to do without one of the three, which should go first?" "Do without the weapons." "And if one had to do without one of the remaining two?" "Do without the food. From of old, death has come to all; but a people without trust cannot stand."

子贡问政。子曰：「足食，足兵，民信之矣。」子贡曰：「必不得已而去，于斯三者何先？」曰：「去兵。」……曰：「去食。自古皆有死，民无信不立。」

Anchor (core).

The ordering is the content, and it is severe. Weapons go first. Then food. What cannot go is that the people trust the government.

And the reason given is not sentimental: from of old, death has come to all — a state that has lost the people's trust does not exist as a state, whatever it still has in its granaries.

Note what this does to 8.9. A man who holds that a government without the people's trust cannot stand is not the source of a doctrine about keeping them ignorant. Trust requires that they be in a position to judge.

12.8

Ji Zicheng said: "In a noble person it is substance that matters. What need of refinement?" Zigong said: "What a pity that you should speak so of the noble person! A team of four horses cannot overtake the tongue. Refinement is as substance; substance is as refinement. The hide of a tiger or leopard, stripped of hair, is as the hide of a dog or sheep."

棘子成曰：「君子质而已矣，何以文为？」子贡曰：「惜乎，夫子之说君子也！驷不及舌。文犹质也，质犹文也。虎豹之鞟，犹犬羊之鞟。」

Anchor (probable) — and one of the best things a student says in the book.

Zigong defends refinement against a plain-substance argument, and does it with an image: strip a tiger's hide and a dog's hide of their hair, and you cannot tell them apart.

Which is 6.18 defended by a student — refinement and substance in balance, neither sufficient. And the aside — a team of four horses cannot overtake the tongue — is a rebuke for saying it at all, in the register of 11.6's white sceptre.

12.9

Duke Ai asked You Ruo: "The year is one of dearth and there is not enough for expenditure. What should be done?" You Ruo replied: "Why not the tithe?" "With two parts in ten I still have not enough. How could I use the tithe?" You Ruo replied: "If the people have enough, with whom will the ruler not have enough? If the people have not enough, with whom will the ruler have enough?"

哀公问于有若曰：「年饥，用不足，如之何？」有若对曰：「盍彻乎？」曰：「二，吾犹不足，如之何其彻也？」对曰：「百姓足，君孰与不足？百姓不足，君孰与足？」

Anchor (core) — though the words are You Ruo's.

A duke in a famine year asks how to raise revenue, and is told to halve the tax rate.

And when he objects, the answer reframes what "the ruler's sufficiency" even means: if the people have enough, with whom will the ruler not have enough? The ruler's account and the people's are not two accounts.

This anchors despite being a student's — as several passages do (19.3, 19.19, 18.2). What matters is whether the passage carries the structure, and this one carries it exactly: the endpoint is what the people have, and the ruler's position is derived from it rather than opposed to it.

12.10

Zizhang asked how to raise virtue and detect confusion. The Master said: "Hold faithfulness and good faith first, and move toward what is right — that is raising virtue. You love a man and want him to live; you hate him and want him dead. Having wanted him to live and also wanted him dead — that is confusion."

子张问崇德辨惑。子曰：「主忠信，徙义，崇德也。爱之欲其生，恶之欲其死。既欲其生，又欲其死，是惑也。」

Anchor (core).

The diagnosis of confusion is exact and unusual. Not error, not ignorance — wanting incompatible things about the same person, according to how one currently feels about him.

Which locates the fault precisely: the attitude has been tied to one's own liking rather than to what the man is. The feelings vary, so the wants contradict.

And the cure is in the first clause: 徙义 — move toward what is right. Attach the judgment to what is fitting rather than to how one feels. (With 4.3, only one with ren can like and dislike people — liking that is not hostage to one's own wants reads people accurately.)

12.11

Duke Jing of Qi asked about government. Confucius replied: "Let the ruler be a ruler, the minister a minister, the father a father, the son a son." The Duke said: "Excellent! Truly, if the ruler is not a ruler, the minister not a minister, the father not a father, the son not a son — though I had grain, would I get to eat it?"

齐景公问政于孔子。孔子对曰：「君君，臣臣，父父，子子。」公曰：「善哉！信如君不君，臣不臣，父不父，子不子，虽有粟，吾得而食诸？」

Anchor (core).

Each phrase is a demand that a position be filled, not a statement of rank. Let the ruler be a ruler — let him do a ruler's work. And it is said to a ruler, first.

The misreading we oppose: the reversal of addressee, by which obligations stated to a ruler became a code of one-way obedience for subjects, and this chapter became scriptural warrant for the three bonds.

Note what has to happen for that reading to work. Let the ruler be a ruler has to be read as the ruler is the ruler — a demand converted into a fact. And the man who set the demand first was in a position where the ruler was the one failing to fill it.

Read with 3.19: the ruler employs by ritual, the minister serves with loyalty — and there too the ruler's clause comes first.

12.12

The Master said: "The one who could decide a case on a single party's word — that would be You!" Zilu never let a promise wait overnight.

子曰：「片言可以折狱者，其由也与！」子路无宿诺。

Anchor (probable). Neither pure praise nor pure criticism.

The praise is real: his word carried, and he decided quickly (with 5.6, he has ascended to the hall; 11.14).

And the thing to watch is in the same sentence. On a single party's word — deciding a dispute having heard one side is exactly what a decisive man is prone to, and exactly what 12.6 warns about (the accusation that cuts to the flesh and demands an immediate answer).

The chapter names a strength and its adjacent failure without separating them — which is how this book's assessments generally work.

12.13

"In hearing lawsuits I am like other men. What is necessary is to bring it about that there are no lawsuits."

子曰：「听讼，吾犹人也。必也使无讼乎！」

Anchor (core).

The "if it must be" form again (with 13.3, 3.7), and it goes to the root: judging disputes well is dealing with what has already happened.

And note that he does not claim to be good at it. I am like other men — the skill is disclaimed, and the aim is placed elsewhere.

12.14

Zizhang asked about government. The Master said: "Occupy the office without weariness; carry out its business with full effort."

子张问政。子曰：「居之无倦，行之以忠。」

Anchor (probable). Two clauses, no elaboration.

Consistent with everything around it — but a short prescription without a visible occasion, and it is graded accordingly.

12.15

"Broadly learned in culture, bound in by ritual, one may thereby not go astray."

子曰：「博学于文，约之以礼，亦可以弗畔矣夫！」

Anchor (probable). The same sentence as 6.27 (which has the noble person at its head).

Two transmission streams for one line — which is a positive signal in this reading. See 6.27 for the reading, and 9.11 for the same method described from the student's side.

12.16

"The noble person brings out what is good in others, and does not bring out what is bad. The small man does the reverse."

子曰：「君子成人之美，不成人之恶。小人反是。」

Anchor (core).

The positive definition of what a noble person does to other people, and it is the whole of this reading in one line: complete what is good in them.

Not: be good to them. Not: judge them accurately. Bring the good in them to completion — which requires that the completion be theirs. (With 6.30's wishing to stand, help others to stand; 7.28's I approve his coming forward.)

And the negative half is not filler. Does not bring out what is bad — one can complete a person's worst as easily as their best, and the small man does.

12.17

Ji Kangzi asked about government. Confucius replied: "To govern is to be straight. If you lead by being straight, who will dare not to be straight?"

季康子问政于孔子。孔子对曰：「政者，正也。子帅以正，孰敢不正？」

Anchor (core).

A pun that is also a definition. 政 (govern) and 正 (be straight) are near-homophones, and the answer collapses one into the other.

And the second clause turns it onto the questioner. If you lead by being straight — the man asking how to manage people is told the management begins with himself.

12.18

Ji Kangzi was troubled by thieves and asked Confucius about it. Confucius replied: "If you yourself were without desire, they would not steal though you paid them to."

季康子患盗，问于孔子。孔子对曰：「苟子之不欲，虽赏之不窃。」

Anchor (core).

Ji Kangzi treats theft as a problem in the population. The answer relocates it.

And the phrasing is deliberately extreme: they would not steal though you paid them to. Not "less theft" — no theft, even under inducement. The claim is that the appetite at the top is what generates the appetite below.

Said to the head of the family that had taken the state, and whose steward — his own former student — was squeezing the people for them (11.17).

12.19

Ji Kangzi asked: "Suppose I were to kill those without the way, to bring on those with it. How would that be?" Confucius replied: "You are governing — what need have you of killing? If you desire what is good, the people will be good. The virtue of the noble person is the wind; the virtue of the small man is the grass. The grass must bend when the wind blows over it."

季康子问政于孔子曰：「如杀无道，以就有道，何如？」孔子对曰：「子为政，焉用杀？子欲善，而民善矣。君子之德风，小人之德草，草上之风，必偃。」

Anchor (core).

Three questions from Ji Kangzi, three redirections — and this is the third and hardest, because what is proposed is killing.

What need have you of killing? The proposal is refused before it is analysed.

And the wind-and-grass image is often read as a claim about the docility of the common people. Read in place, it is doing something else: it is telling the man who wanted to execute people that what he is will propagate whether he intends it or not. If you desire what is good, the people will be good — the causal claim is about the ruler's own condition, and the image says he cannot opt out of it.

The wind does not get to choose whether the grass bends.

12.20

Zizhang asked: "What must a gentleman be like before he may be called accomplished?" The Master said: "What do you mean by accomplished?" Zizhang replied: "Certain to be renowned in the state, certain to be renowned in the family." The Master said: "That is renown, not accomplishment. The accomplished man is solid and straight and loves what is right; he weighs what is said and observes the expression; he takes thought and defers to others. … The man of renown assumes the appearance of ren and acts otherwise, and dwells in this without misgiving."

子张问：「士何如斯可谓之达矣？」子曰：「何哉，尔所谓达者？」……「夫达也者，质直而好义，察言而观色，虑以下人……夫闻也者，色取仁而行违，居之不疑。」

Anchor (core).

The exchange begins with a question back: what do you mean by accomplished? — and the student's answer reveals that he meant renown.

Which is the whole chapter: the student had the wrong thing in mind, and did not know it.

And the distinction drawn is between substance and appearance, made precise. The accomplished man is described by what he does — solid, straight, weighs what is said, watches expressions, defers. The man of renown is described by a gap: assumes the appearance of ren and acts otherwise — and, crucially, dwells in this without misgiving.

The last clause is the sharpest. The counterfeit is not a man knowingly deceiving. It is a man entirely comfortable in the discrepancy.

12.21

Fan Chi, walking with him below the rain-altar, said: "May I ask about raising virtue, correcting badness, and detecting confusion?" The Master said: "An excellent question! To put the work first and the gain after — is that not raising virtue? To attack one's own badness and not the badness of others — is that not correcting badness? Because of one morning's anger to forget oneself and involve one's parents — is that not confusion?"

樊迟从游于舞雩之下，曰：「敢问崇德、修慝、辨惑。」子曰：「善哉问！先事后得，非崇德与？攻其恶，无攻人之恶，非修慝与？一朝之忿，忘其身，以及其亲，非惑与？」

Anchor (core).

The same three subjects Zizhang asked about at 12.10 — and the answers are different, because Fan Chi is different.

Zizhang, who wanted renown, was given: attach yourself to what is right, and stop wanting incompatible things. Fan Chi is given three concrete disciplines, each in the form of a question he can check himself against.

And all three are aimed low and practically: work before payment; your own faults and not other people's; and a morning's temper that costs you your family.

(And note the second: attacking others' badness is what Zizhang would be inclined to. The prescription for one man is the diagnosis of another.)

12.22

Fan Chi asked about ren. The Master said: "Love people." He asked about knowing. The Master said: "Know people." Fan Chi did not understand. The Master said: "Raise up the straight and set them over the crooked, and you can make the crooked straight." Fan Chi withdrew and saw Zixia. "A moment ago I saw the Master and asked about knowing. He said: raise up the straight and set them over the crooked — you can make the crooked straight. What does it mean?" Zixia said: "How rich in meaning! When Shun had the empire, he chose Gao Yao from the multitude, and those without ren went far away."

樊迟问仁。子曰：「爱人。」问知。子曰：「知人。」樊迟未达。子曰：「举直错诸枉，能使枉者直。」樊迟退，见子夏……子夏曰：「富哉言乎！……」

Anchor (core).

The two shortest definitions in the book: ren is to love people; knowing is to know people. Both are about persons, and they are matched — the caring and the seeing.

And when Fan Chi does not follow, the answer is not repeated more slowly. It is given again in a different form — an example from administration.

And then the last movement, which is the chapter's real interest. Fan Chi still does not have it, so he goes and asks another student — and Zixia supplies the historical case that makes it land.

The teaching completed itself sideways. He did not chase Fan Chi down with a third explanation. (With 7.8: if I raise one corner and they do not come back with the other three, I do not go on.)

And 12.22's phrase — can make the crooked straight — is the deeper version of 2.19, where the same policy produced only submission. Here placement does not merely select. It corrects.

12.23

Zigong asked about friends. The Master said: "Advise them loyally and guide them well. If you cannot, then stop. Do not disgrace yourself."

子贡问友。子曰：「忠告而善道之，不可则止，毋自辱焉。」

Anchor (core).

不可则止 — stop where you cannot. The same clause as at 11.24 (serving a ruler) and compressed by Ziyou at 4.26.

And it applies to urging someone toward the good. Even that may not be pressed. Advise fully, guide well — and when it does not take, stop.

Which follows from the whole structure: if the acknowledgment has to be theirs, pressing past refusal is not more help. It is the attempt to produce by force what only they can produce.

12.24

Zengzi said: "The noble person gathers friends by his learning, and by his friends supports his ren."

曾子曰：「君子以文会友，以友辅仁。」

Non-anchor, self-attributed.

Nothing wrong with it, and it is consistent (with 7.21, 4.25). Two clauses of good sense, with no occasion and nothing at stake in them.

Book 12 in sum. Fifteen core anchors.

And two structures are established here that the rest of the work uses.

The three answers about ren, on one page — which is not a difficulty in the text to be explained away, but the method displayed by the compilers, who could easily have kept only one.

And the ruler's redirection, three times to Ji Kangzi: to govern is to be straight; if you were without desire; what need have you of killing? In each case a question about managing the people is returned to the person asking it.

Book 13 · 子路 Zi Lu

The book of government, and the argument in it is single. Nine chapters make the same move from different directions: what a ruler is determines what happens, and no instrument of management substitutes for it.

It also contains the sentence this reading treats as the sharpest political line in the Analects (13.15, on the ruler no one dares contradict), the hardest case of what "straight" means (13.18, the father and the sheep), and the definition of harmony that separates it from agreement (13.23).

13.1

Zilu asked about government. The Master said: "Go before them; work them hard." He asked for more. "Do not tire of it."

子路问政。子曰：「先之劳之。」请益。曰：「无倦。」

Anchor (core).

Three words, and both halves are about the governor. Go before them — lead by being in front, not by issuing from behind (12.17, 2.1). Work them hard reads, in this line, as work at it before them — the exertion is his.

And when asked for more, he gives one clause and no elaboration: do not tire of it. The addition is duration, not content.

13.2

Zhonggong, being steward to the Ji family, asked about government. The Master said: "Put the officers in charge first; pardon small faults; raise up those of ability." "How shall I know those of ability, so as to raise them?" "Raise those you know. Those you do not — will others let them go?"

仲弓为季氏宰，问政。子曰：「先有司，赦小过，举贤才。」曰：「焉知贤才而举之？」曰：「举尔所知。尔所不知，人其舍诸？」

Anchor (core).

Three instructions, and the third gets a follow-up question that produces the best line in the chapter.

Zhonggong's objection is practical: how can I raise able men I have not identified? And the answer removes the anxiety without answering the question as posed: raise those you know — and the ones you do not know will not be lost, because others will not overlook them.

Which is a statement about the system rather than about him. He does not have to be the sole judge of everyone. (Compare pardon small faults — the same disposition: do not attempt total control.)

13.3

Zilu said: "If the ruler of Wei were waiting for you to govern, what would you put first?" The Master said: "It would certainly be to make names correct." Zilu said: "Is that so? How wide of the mark! Why this correcting?" The Master said: "How uncouth you are, You! Where a noble person does not know, he leaves a blank. If names are not correct, speech does not follow; if speech does not follow, affairs are not completed; if affairs are not completed, ritual and music do not flourish; if ritual and music do not flourish, punishments miss the mark; if punishments miss the mark, the people have nowhere to put hand or foot. Therefore the noble person names only what can be spoken of, and speaks only of what can be carried out."

子路曰：「卫君待子而为政，子将奚先？」子曰：「必也正名乎！」……「名不正，则言不顺；言不顺，则事不成……则民无所措手足。故君子名之必可言也，言之必可行也。」

Split judgment: the core anchors; the middle chain does not.

The core is it would certainly be to make names correct — and the closing clause: the noble person names only what can be spoken of, and speaks only of what can be carried out. Naming and doing are bound together; what cannot be carried out should not be said. (With where a noble person does not know, he leaves a blank — the same leave a gap discipline as 2.18, 15.26.)

And there is a live case standing behind it. The ruler of Wei was a grandson holding the throne against his own father — a situation in which no name fitted anyone.

The middle does not anchor. The six-step chain — names → speech → affairs → ritual and music → punishments → the people — is a deduction of a kind found nowhere else in the text, and it runs the argument to a terminus in penal law.

Compare 3.2, where the same work is done in eight characters and a question: attended by the lords of the land, the Son of Heaven majestic — what has that to do with the halls of three families? That is what rectification of names looks like when he does it. The chain is an elaboration, and its endpoint — punishments — is not where he takes anything else.

The reading we oppose: taking this chain as a doctrine that correct nomenclature is enforced by the penal system. It is the one place in the text where an argument arrives at punishment, and it arrives by a route that appears nowhere else.

13.4

Fan Chi asked to learn about growing grain. The Master said: "I am not as good as an old farmer." He asked to learn about growing vegetables. "I am not as good as an old gardener." Fan Chi went out. The Master said: "What a small man Fan Xu is! If those above love ritual, none of the people will dare be irreverent. If they love what is right, none will dare be disobedient. If they love good faith, none will dare hold back the truth. And if it be so, the people from every quarter will come with their children strapped on their backs. What need to grow one's own grain?"

樊迟请学稼。子曰：「吾不如老农。」……子曰：「小人哉，樊须也！……焉用稼？」

Anchor (probable) — with the reading of 小人 stated, because the chapter turns on it.

Read as a mean person, this is a scholar despising manual work, and it sits badly with everything else (6.30's take what is near at hand; his own account of learning menial things because he was poor, 9.6).

Read as one who has not yet become a noble person — the sense this book takes throughout (2.14, 4.11, 4.16) — it is a placement, not contempt: Fan Chi is asking about the wrong level of the problem.

And the answer says which level. The question was how to make people fed. The answer is that what makes people come and stay is what those above them are — and the people will come with their children strapped on their backs is a description of that, not a boast.

Note that he does not claim agricultural expertise he lacks: I am not as good as an old farmer is straightforwardly true and said without embarrassment.

13.5

"A man may recite the three hundred Odes; but give him a governmental charge and he cannot carry it through; send him to the four quarters and he cannot answer for himself. Though he has much, of what use is it?"

子曰：「诵《诗》三百，授之以政，不达；使于四方，不能专对；虽多，亦奚以为？」

Anchor (core).

The test of learning is whether it operates — and both tests named are situations requiring judgment on one's own: carrying through a charge, answering for oneself abroad with no script.

With 6.3 (learning as self-transformation, no books in it), 1.6 (texts come last), 15.3 (not learning much and retaining it). Quantity of retained material is precisely not what is being measured.

13.6

"When his own person is correct, things go without orders. When his own person is not correct, though he orders, he is not obeyed."

子曰：「其身正，不令而行；其身不正，虽令不从。」

Anchor (core).

The plainest statement of the book's political argument. With 12.17, 12.19, 13.13, 2.1 — five formulations of one claim.

And what the claim rules out is worth naming: it removes the possibility of governing by instrument alone. Orders are not a substitute for what the person giving them is; and where he is not correct, the orders do not merely fail — they fail visibly.

13.7

"The governments of Lu and Wei are brothers."

子曰：「鲁、卫之政，兄弟也。」

Non-anchor at the baseline, with a reading recorded.

On its own the line is a general remark with no visible referent.

If the point is that Lu and Wei shared the same disease — the ducal house hollowed out, power lodged with subordinate families (the three houses in Lu; Nanzi and the succession quarrel in Wei) — then it is a diagnosis, and would anchor.

The context does not survive. Baseline judgment stands.

13.8

The Master said of Prince Jing of Wei that he managed a household well. When he first had something, he said: "This is roughly enough." When he had a little more: "This is roughly complete." When he was wealthy: "This is roughly fine."

子谓卫公子荆，「善居室。始有，曰：『苟合矣。』少有，曰：『苟完矣。』富有，曰：『苟美矣。』」

Anchor (probable).

Three stages, and the same word — 苟, roughly, this will do — at every one of them.

What is praised is that he stops at each level. Having a little, it is enough; having more, complete; being rich, fine. The satisfaction does not wait on the next increment.

Which is a small chapter about a large thing: wanting that has no terminus is what makes a man usable by his own appetites (with 5.11, Cheng has desires — how can he be firm?).

13.9

The Master went to Wei, with Ran You driving. The Master said: "How numerous they are!" Ran You said: "Being already numerous, what more should be done?" "Make them prosperous." "Being already prosperous, what more?" "Teach them."

子适卫，冉有仆。子曰：「庶矣哉！」冉有曰：「既庶矣，又何加焉？」曰：「富之。」曰：「既富矣，又何加焉？」曰：「教之。」

Anchor (core). Three stages of government, in order.

Numerous → prosperous → taught. And the order is not decorative.

Teaching comes third — after material sufficiency. A population that is hungry is not in a position to be cultivated, and the sequence says so without saying it. (With 12.9: if the people have enough, with whom will the ruler not have enough?)

And teaching is the terminus rather than the means. It is not there to make them governable; the previous two stages exist so that it becomes possible.

13.10

"If someone were to employ me, in twelve months something could be done; in three years there would be results."

子曰：「苟有用我者，期月而已可也，三年有成。」

Anchor (weak at baseline).

On its own, a statement of confidence.

Placed in the years of travelling between states, looking for a ruler who would employ him and finding none, it reads differently: if someone were to employ me is a conditional that was not met. The confidence and the failure are in the same sentence.

13.11

"'If good men governed a state for a hundred years, cruelty could be overcome and killing done away with.' How true that saying is!"

子曰：「『善人为邦百年，亦可以胜残去杀矣。』诚哉是言也！」

Anchor (probable).

He quotes it and endorses it — and the figure is a hundred years.

Which is the point. Overcoming cruelty and doing away with executions is achievable, and it takes longer than any ruler's lifetime. (With 13.12, 13.17.)

13.12

"Even with a true king, it would require a generation before ren prevailed."

子曰：「如有王者，必世而后仁。」

Anchor (core).

A generation — thirty years — even under the best possible ruler.

And this is the political form of the whole method. Cultivation cannot be accelerated, because what it produces has to be produced by the person themselves. A ruler can create the conditions; he cannot install the result.

Which is also why the alternative is always available and always faster. Coercion works next week. That is its entire appeal, and this chapter is the price of refusing it.

13.13

"If a man can make himself correct, what difficulty will he have in governing? If he cannot make himself correct, how is he to correct others?"

子曰：「苟正其身矣，于从政乎何有？不能正其身，如正人何？」

Anchor (core). The same claim as 13.6, put as two questions.

13.14

Ran Zi returned from court. The Master said: "Why so late?" He replied: "There was government business." The Master said: "It will have been family business. If there had been government business, though I am not in office, I should have heard of it."

冉子退朝。子曰：「何晏也？」对曰：「有政。」子曰：「其事也。如有政，虽不吾以，我也与闻之。」

Anchor (probable).

A distinction insisted on, in a single word. What Ran Qiu was doing at the Ji family's court was 事 — their household affairs — not 政, the business of the state.

And the correction matters because the confusion was the usurpation itself: a private family conducting the state's business as its own. Calling it government business concedes the point.

Which is 13.3's rectification of names, applied on the spot, in one word.

13.15

Duke Ding asked: "Is there a single saying that can make a state prosper?" Confucius replied: "Words cannot be expected to do so much. But there is a saying among men: 'To be a ruler is hard; to be a minister is not easy.' If one knows the difficulty of being a ruler, is that not close to a single saying making a state prosper?" "Is there a single saying that can ruin a state?" "Words cannot be expected to do so much. But there is a saying among men: 'I take no pleasure in being a ruler, except that no one opposes what I say.' If what he says is good and no one opposes it — is that not good? But if what he says is not good and no one opposes it — is that not close to a single saying ruining a state?"

定公问：「一言而可以兴邦，有诸？」……「予无乐乎为君，唯其言而莫予违也。」……「如不善而莫之违也，不几乎一言而丧邦乎？」

Anchor (core). The sharpest political sentence in the Analects.

The thing that ruins a state is named exactly: 莫予违 — no one opposes what I say.

And the reasoning is done properly rather than asserted. If what he says is good and no one opposes, no harm follows. The ruin is conditional: it is when the ruler is wrong and the correction cannot reach him that the state goes.

So what is being described is not tyranny. It is the absence of a correction channel — which is the political form of everything this book says about teaching. A student who never disagrees is no help (11.4). A ruler no one contradicts is the end of the state.

And note the pairing. To be a ruler is hard makes a state prosper; no one opposes me ruins it. The prospering saying is one that keeps the ruler uncertain.

13.16

The Duke of She asked about government. The Master said: "Those near are pleased; those far off come."

叶公问政。子曰：「近者说，远者来。」

Anchor (core).

A test of government, stated as an observable outcome and containing no policy at all.

And it is a test the governed administer. Whether those nearby are content, and whether those elsewhere choose to come — people vote by staying and by arriving. (With 13.4's children strapped on their backs.)

13.17

Zixia, being steward of Jufu, asked about government. The Master said: "Do not want speed; do not look at small advantages. Want speed, and you do not arrive. Look at small advantages, and great affairs are not accomplished."

子夏为莒父宰，问政。子曰：「无欲速，无见小利。欲速则不达，见小利则大事不成。」

Anchor (core).

Two negations, each with its consequence stated.

And want speed and you do not arrive is not a proverb about patience. It is the same claim as 13.12: what is being produced has to grow, and the methods that are fast are fast because they skip the growing.

13.18

The Duke of She said to Confucius: "In our part of the country there is one who is upright: his father stole a sheep, and the son bore witness against him." Confucius said: "In our part of the country the upright are different from this. The father conceals for the son, and the son conceals for the father — uprightness lies therein."

叶公语孔子曰：「吾党有直躬者，其父攘羊，而子证之。」孔子曰：「吾党之直者异于是：父为子隐，子为父隐，直在其中矣。」

Anchor (core) — and the chapter most in need of careful statement.

What this is not. It is not a licence to obstruct justice, not a claim that family loyalty overrides law, and not a general permission to lie for one's relatives. None of that is in the sentence.

What is being contested is what upright means.

The Duke's man is upright in the sense of reporting what is the case regardless of relation. The counter-claim is that uprightness is not information-transmission — it is 5.24's what is, is; what is not, is not, applied to a person who is standing in a relation.

And the specific point is that a son who testifies against his father has not thereby been more truthful. He has disowned a relation that is a fact about him. The concealment named is not fabrication of evidence; it is not being the instrument by which one's own father is taken.

And the phrase is uprightness lies therein — 在其中 — the by-product form (with joy lies therein, 7.15; a salary lies therein, 2.18). It is not "this is uprightness." It is: what uprightness is, is somewhere in here.

A reading we oppose: using this line to underwrite the concealment of official wrongdoing by kin, which requires reading 隐 as active obstruction and extending a claim about a father and a sheep to a doctrine about power. Neither move is in the text.

13.19

Fan Chi asked about ren. The Master said: "In private life, be respectful; in handling business, be reverent; with others, do your utmost. Even among the tribes, these may not be abandoned."

樊迟问仁。子曰：「居处恭，执事敬，与人忠。虽之夷狄，不可弃也。」

Anchor (core).

Fan Chi's third answer about ren — with love people (12.22) and put the difficulty first and the gain after (6.22).

Three plain dispositions, each attached to a domain: private life, work, other people. Operable, and aimed at the man who needed things he could act on.

And the closing clause is doing something particular. Even among the tribes — the disposition is not a local custom that stops at the border. (With 9.14: if a noble person lived among them, what crudeness would there be?)

13.20

Zigong asked: "What must one be to be called a gentleman?" The Master said: "One who in his conduct has a sense of shame, and who, sent to the four quarters, does not disgrace his ruler's commission." "May I ask about the next grade?" "One whose clan calls him filial and whose village calls him fraternal." "And the next?" "One whose word is certainly to be trusted and whose action is certainly carried through — a hard, obstinate small man. But he may be the next." "What of those now in government?" "Ah! Peck-and-hamper men — not worth taking into account."

子贡问曰：「何如斯可谓之士矣？」……曰：「言必信，行必果，硁硁然小人哉！抑亦可以为次矣。」……「噫！斗筲之人，何足算也！」

Anchor (core). Three grades, and the third is the one worth the attention.

First grade: a sense of shame in one's own conduct, and competence under a commission. The shame is self-administered (with 1.13, where shame becomes an account others keep).

And the third grade — whose word is certainly to be trusted and whose action is certainly carried through — is called a hard, obstinate small man.

Which is startling if one has taken "always keep your word" to be the point of this book. The objection is in 硁硁 — hard, unyielding, a stone struck. Keeping one's word regardless of what turns out to be the case is the same failure as any other rule applied without judgment (with 4.10, no predisposition for and none against; 9.30, weighing at the top of the ladder).

And it is still a grade. He does not throw the man out — third, but on the list. Those actually holding office rank below all three.

13.21

"Since I cannot get men of the middle way to associate with, I must have the ardent and the scrupulous! The ardent go forward and take hold; the scrupulous refrain from doing certain things."

子曰：「不得中行而与之，必也狂狷乎！狂者进取，狷者有所不为也。」

Anchor (core).

The middle way is preferred and unavailable — and what is chosen instead is not the moderate compromise but two extremes.

Because each of them has something. The ardent will move. The scrupulous will refuse. A man in the middle of the range with neither quality has nothing to work with.

Which is the practical form of 11.16 (to go too far is the same as not going far enough): both deviations are deviations, and both are workable material. Flatness is not.

13.22

"The southerners have a saying: 'A man without constancy cannot be a shaman or a physician.' Well said! He whose virtue is not constant will meet with disgrace." The Master said: "It is simply that such a man does not consult the oracle."

子曰：「南人有言曰：『人而无恒，不可以作巫医。』善夫！『不恒其德，或承之羞。』」子曰：「不占而已矣。」

Anchor (probable).

Constancy is set as the floor beneath every other capacity — a man without it cannot manage even the trades that most obviously require staying with something.

And the closing line is dry: for such a man there is no need to consult the oracle. The outcome does not require divination.

13.23

"The noble person harmonizes and does not merely agree; the small man agrees and does not harmonize."

子曰：「君子和而不同，小人同而不和。」

Anchor (core).

The distinction is between two ways of being with other people, and it turns entirely on whether difference survives.

和 — harmony — requires distinct parts. Things that are identical cannot be in harmony; they are merely the same. 同 — sameness — is agreement reached by the parties becoming alike.

So the noble person's connection preserves the other as other, and the small man's dissolves the difference. Which is 2.14's joins with all and takes no side in another form — and, put politically, it is 13.15: a court where everyone agrees has achieved 同, and no one can correct the ruler.

13.24

Zigong asked: "What would you say of a man whom all his neighbours like?" The Master said: "That is not enough." "What of one whom all his neighbours dislike?" "That is not enough either. Better: those in the village who are good like him, and those who are bad dislike him."

子贡问曰：「乡人皆好之，何如？」子曰：「未可也。」「乡人皆恶之，何如？」子曰：「未可也。不如乡人之善者好之，其不善者恶之。」

Anchor (core).

Neither universal approval nor universal disapproval settles anything — and the reason is that both count heads.

The test given counts who. Being liked by the good and disliked by the bad is a reading; being liked by everyone is not.

And universal approval is specifically suspect — a man everyone likes has probably not stood anywhere (with the xiangyuan, the village worthy, at 17.13).

13.25

"The noble person is easy to serve and hard to please: try to please him otherwise than by the way, and he is not pleased; but in employing men, he uses them according to their capacity. The small man is hard to serve and easy to please: please him even not by the way, and he is pleased; but in employing men, he requires everything of them."

子曰：「君子易事而难说也……及其使人也，器之。小人难事而易说也……及其使人也，求备焉。」

Anchor (core).

A double inversion, and the second half is the substance.

Uses them according to their capacity — the same word 器 as a noble person is not a vessel (2.12) and you are a vessel (5.3), turned outward: he sees what each person is and puts them where that works.

Against requires everything of them — demanding of everyone the same completeness, which is what a man does when he has no capacity to read people.

And the paradox resolves: the one who is hard to please is easy to work for because his pleasure tracks the work rather than the flattery — so one can know where one stands.

13.26

"The noble person is composed and not arrogant; the small man is arrogant and not composed."

子曰：「君子泰而不骄，小人骄而不泰。」

Anchor (core).

And the two are causally related, which is the point. Composure that is settled inwardly has no need to display; arrogance is what display looks like when the composure is absent.

With 7.36 (composed and at ease / always full of anxiety), and 8.11, where arrogance and grudging cancel every gift a man has.

13.27

"Firm, resolute, plain, halting in speech — these are near to ren."

子曰：「刚、毅、木、讷，近仁。」

Anchor (core).

The positive counterpart of 1.3 (clever words and an ingratiating face — seldom is there ren). One scale, read from both ends.

And the qualities named are not virtues but absences of manipulation. Firm — nothing to grip him by (5.11). Resolute. Plain — unornamented. Halting in speech — not smooth.

None of these is admirable in itself. What they have in common is that a man like this is not managing you.

13.28

Zilu asked: "What must one be to be called a gentleman?" The Master said: "Earnest and exacting with one another, and yet cheerful — such a one may be called a gentleman. Earnest and exacting with friends; cheerful with brothers."

子路问曰：「何如斯可谓之士矣？」子曰：「切切偲偲，怡怡如也，可谓士矣。朋友切切偲偲，兄弟怡怡。」

Anchor (core).

The same question Zigong asked at 13.20, and a different answer — because a different man is asking.

Zigong, the diplomat and operator, was given a sense of shame and the standard of a commission. Zilu, who is blunt, is given: be exacting and cheerful, and know which is for whom.

And the second half is the correction hidden in it: exacting with friends, cheerful with brothers. Zilu's fault is that he brings the same manner everywhere.

13.29

"When good men have taught the people for seven years, they may be sent to war."

子曰：「善人教民七年，亦可以即戎矣。」

Anchor (probable).

Seven years of teaching before the people may be sent to fight — and the number is doing the same work as the hundred years of 13.11 and the generation of 13.12.

Read with 13.30, which supplies the reason.

13.30

"To send the people to war untaught is to throw them away."

子曰：「以不教民战，是谓弃之。」

Anchor (core).

弃 — to discard, to abandon. Nine characters, and the whole of the political argument is in them.

Sending untaught people to fight treats them as material rather than as people — expended for an end they were never brought into. And the word chosen is not harm or waste but throw away: what is done to something that was never regarded as anything.

With 6.4 (help the urgent, do not add to the wealth of the rich), 12.19 (what need have you of killing?), and 11.17 (a student disowned for squeezing the people).

Book 13 in sum. Nineteen core anchors — and one argument, made from nine directions.

What a ruler is determines what happens (13.6, 13.13, 12.17). Instruments do not substitute for it — not orders, not punishment, not speed.
